# Detailed plan: cap tracked MCP tasks per server, unfinished ones included, through one way in

> Written on main `31c1357` (dev0, a team host), worktree `pmcp-338`, branch
> `plan/338-task-cap`. Every number below was measured on that tree, or on
> the spike of this plan applied to it (local branch `spike/338`, commit
> `6db13ca`, rev 13; never pushed). This PR carries only this file.
>
> **Revision 13 (2026-10-05), on main `31c1357`.** It answers board round 12
> on rev 12 (`b8b8c40`). The external seats returned DEGRADED: the bundle
> (534 KB) exceeded the transport bound. Rev 13 is therefore also a trim:
> the per-round narrative of revs 1–12 is collapsed into *History* below,
> superseded designs and retired mutants are one line each, and the eight
> historical repro scripts are replaced by the tests that carry their
> findings. The current rules, design decisions, acceptance criteria,
> mutation table and verbatim bodies are in full.
>
> **Claude F001 (blocking, a regression against main): an abort dropped
> owed tasks on other servers.** A forced refresh owes `alpha::a1`,
> `beta::b1` and `beta::b2` and waits on `a1`'s cancel; two new beta tasks
> evict `b1` and `b2` into its custody; a forced disconnect of *alpha*
> cancels the refresh's in-flight request, the refresh ends with
> `CancelledError`, and its custody was released with them. Nobody had
> cancelled them, nobody tracked them: a retry missed them and a disconnect
> of beta without `force` was not refused. Reproduced on rev 12 with the
> seat's falsifier as written: `(('beta', 'b2'), [('alpha', 'a1'),
> ('alpha', 'a1')], [('beta', 'f0'), ('beta', 'f1')])`. Main kept both in
> the registry.
>
> **The rule now: releasing a custody never drops a task that was not
> cancelled; an abort is not a discharge** (Design decision 16). However a
> teardown ends -- success, its caller cancelled, a downstream error, or a
> forced disconnect of another server -- every owed record its custody
> still holds that pmcp tracks nowhere else, that is unfinished and that is
> on its server's current connection, goes back into the registry, oldest
> first. It is tracked and retriable again; it is under the caps again, like
> any record (compared honestly with main in Design decision 16). The seat's
> option (a), skipping an entry whose future a disconnect cancelled, is not
> taken: on 3.10 the awaiting task cannot tell that from its own
> cancellation without changing `_send_request`, and the release rule
> already gives the outcome on every abort path.
>
> **Also in rev 13:** the overlap harness models aborts precisely, with no
> wholesale exemption, and adds a forced disconnect of the other server; N1
> (a pin's restore can evict at request start), N2 (a chain re-sends
> cancels), N3 (the harness docstring on main's residual) and N4 (a
> behavioural test of the refresh gate and counts on a custody-held task);
> mutants M114–M118; the CHANGELOG states the release rule.
>
> **Considered and not adopted: no custody at all** ("a non-terminal task is
> never evicted; a hard bound on non-terminal records with a refusal").
> It would make #338's flood (a downstream reporting `working` for any
> number of tasks) a refusal instead of an eviction: every record path
> (`invoke`, `tasks_get`, `tasks_list`) would need a refusal outcome, and a
> refused `invoke` record loses its default redaction and its context
> exactly as an eviction does. It does not remove the class either: a
> teardown still needs its obligation fixed at request time, and the
> refusal bound is a second cap whose interaction with pins is the round-9
> and round-10 problem again. The custody plus the release rule keeps the
> one cap, and every rule below is a local check.

## History

One row per board round: the finding, and how the current design answers
it. Spikes: rev 1 `ffee47e`, 2 `3bb5b72`, 3 `812af01`, 4 `fd6c342`, 5
`efc9bb6`, 6 `e4e9044`, 7 `879b2ba`, 8 `212e9d6`, 9 `39cccd7`, 10 `fd740c0`,
11 `8ddb584`, 12 `6062cfe`.

| Round (on rev) | Finding | Resolved by (current rule) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 (rev 1) | F001: a record evicted during a forced teardown made the disconnect fail (`Task not found`) | the teardown cancels an evicted owed task (DD 9, 16) |
| 2 (rev 2) | rev 2 skipped such a task, leaving it running remotely | cancel it, through one sender `_send_task_cancel` |
| 3 (rev 3) | a re-tracked context-less record shadowed the held context | `_record_task` merges with the held record (DD 14, 16) |
| 4 (rev 4) | codex: the teardown sent an old connection's id and context to a replacement | state is bound to its connection; `_task_client(bound_to=)` (DD 11) |
| 4 (rev 4) | a post-await record dropped the context its caller held | `requestor_context` keyword-only, no default; `CONTEXT_FLOW` pinned |
| 5 (rev 5) | handlers looked a task up by key after an await, reading a replacement's | `TaskReply`: a handler uses this reply's record (DD 12) |
| 6 (rev 6) | merge before the ownership check; a fallback resolved by name | ownership first; one connection per request, captured at entry (DD 13) |
| 7 (rev 7) | a late reply wrote an older context over a newer one | supplied, else the existing record's (DD 14) |
| 8 (rev 8) | the same race in a third order (newer call, eviction, late reply) | a record with a request in flight is pinned, never evicted (DD 15) |
| 9 (rev 9) | overlapping forced disconnects each pinned a larger snapshot (100 → 1,101) | a teardown pins only its in-flight entry; what it owes is held outside the caps (DD 16). Rev 10's per-server lock is gone |
| 10 (rev 10) | codex: a refresh missed an owed task evicted before its turn; the bound failed when requests finished | the obligation is fixed in the helper's first statement; the registry bound is in live requests (DD 16) |
| 11 (rev 11) | claude/grok: a custody-held task was invisible to other teardowns and gates; codex: a poll on one lost its context when the custody was released | one source, `get_tracked_tasks`; a pin restores a custody's record (DD 16) |
| 12 (rev 12) | claude: an abort by a disconnect of another server dropped owed tasks | releasing a custody returns every uncancelled owed task (DD 16) |

## Task

Consiliency/pmcp#338. The claude seat found it while reviewing
Consiliency/pmcp#337, the #298 implementation, and it is pre-existing on
main.

- **The cap is shared.** pmcp keeps at most 100 *finished* task records, and
  that one cap covers every server. A downstream whose tasks a caller lists
  can fill it and evict another server's finished tasks.
- **Unfinished tasks are not capped at all.** A downstream can keep any
  number of records alive by reporting `status: "working"`. Since #298 the
  same is true of a status pmcp cannot use, which is now recorded as `null`
  and so is never "finished".

The issue suggests the fix: a per-server cap covering finished and unfinished
tasks, eviction within that server first, and a global ceiling as a backstop.
Eviction stays in record order, as #337 established.

It also asks for **three wording corrections** to #337's CHANGELOG entry.
They are verified against the code in *Documentation impact*.

The brief adds four requirements:
- derive every record path from the code, not from the issue;
- prefer one choke point that every path goes through over a check on each
  path;
- add a structural guard if feasible;
- prove the patches embed.

## Research summary

### Every path that records a task (derived from the code)

`ClientManager._tasks` is the only task store. On main it is a plain `dict`,
and the only line that inserts into it is in `_record_task`
(`manager.py:1813`, `self._tasks[(server_name, task_info.task_id)] = record`).
`McpTaskRecord(` is constructed only there (`manager.py:1796`); `rg
'McpTaskRecord\(' src/` has no other hit. So the record paths are exactly the
callers of `_record_task`, found with `rg -n '_record_task\(' src/`:

| # | Caller (`client/manager.py`) | Downstream request | Gateway tool that reaches it | What is recorded |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `call_tool` (`:3990`) | `tools/call` with `task` | `gateway.invoke` | the reply's `task` (or a top-level `taskId`) |
| 2 | `list_tasks` (`:4037`) | `tasks/list` | `gateway.tasks_list` | **every** listed task, one record each |
| 3 | `get_task` (`:4065`) | `tasks/get` | `gateway.tasks_get` | the reply |
| 4 | `get_task_result` (`:4090`) | `tasks/result` | `gateway.tasks_result` | the reply's `task`, when it has one |
| 4b | `get_task_result` → `get_task` (`:4092`) | `tasks/result`, then `tasks/get` | `gateway.tasks_result` | a reply without a `task` falls back to path 3 |
| 5 | `cancel_task` (`:4131`) | `tasks/cancel` | `gateway.tasks_cancel`, `disconnect_server(force)`, `cancel_active_tasks` | the reply |
| 5b | `cancel_task` (`:4123-4130`) | `tasks/cancel` | as 5 | pmcp builds a `cancelled` record itself when the reply has no task |

**Notifications are not a record path.** `_handle_downstream_notification`
(`:2157`) recognises exactly three methods, the three `*/list_changed`, and
returns `False` for anything else. `notifications/tasks/status` therefore
records nothing. The new module pins this
(`test_no_downstream_notification_records_a_task`).

**Nothing else writes.** The other uses of `_tasks` are reads
(`get_task_record`, `get_tracked_tasks`, `get_active_tasks`) and two removals:
- `_remove_server_indexes(drop_tasks=True)` (`:1643`) drops one server's
  records on a disconnect. Its five callers are `:1475`, `:2454`, `:2804`,
  `:3809` and `:3865`. The reconcile at `:2422` passes `drop_tasks=False`
  and keeps them.
- `self._tasks.clear()` (`:3741`) runs at shutdown.

### What hangs on a record (why eviction matters, and to whom)

Read from `manager.py` and `tools/handlers.py` on `31c1357`:

| Consumer | Uses the record for | When the record is gone |
|---|---|---|
| `cancel_task` (`:4109-4113`) | refuses without one | `(False, None, "Task not found: <server>::<id>")`. **Nothing is sent downstream.** |
| `disconnect_server` (`:1385-1398`) | refuses an unforced disconnect while `get_active_tasks(name)` is non-empty; a forced one sends `tasks/cancel` for each | not counted: not waited for, not cancelled |
| `cancel_active_tasks` (`:1865`) | same list | same |
| `get_task` / `get_task_result` (`:4051`, `:4076`) | reuse the stored `requestor_context` when the caller sends none | the downstream is still asked, without the stored context |
| `gateway.invoke` (`handlers.py:1664-1678`) | reads the record straight after `call_tool`; **default redaction is `task_info is not None`** | output is not redacted by default |
| `gateway.tasks_list` (`handlers.py:6021-6026`) | prefers the record | falls back to `McpTaskInfo(**task)` from the listing: no loss |
| `gateway.tasks_result` (`handlers.py:6118`) | `task` in the output | `task: null`; the result itself is unaffected |

Two consequences shape the design:
- **The record a path just stored must survive that same path.**
  `gateway.invoke` reads it back to choose its redaction. With "finished
  first", this is a real trap: a bucket of 100 `working` records receiving one
  `completed` would otherwise evict the new record, which is the only finished
  one.
- **An unfinished record carries behaviour, and a finished one carries a
  cache.** A finished record holds a final status the downstream still serves
  through `tasks/get`. An unfinished record is what `tasks_cancel`, the
  disconnect refusal and the stored `requestor_context` depend on.

### The repro (main `31c1357`)

On main, one server listing 500 finished tasks evicted another server's
finished tasks (the cap was shared), and a downstream reporting `working`
for any number of tasks was never capped. Both are now tests:
`test_one_server_listing_tasks_never_evicts_another_servers` and
`test_an_unfinished_flood_is_capped_at_the_newest_100`, red on main.

### The test surface this touches

`rg -n '_max_terminal_tasks|\._tasks\[' tests/` on `31c1357` finds 7 sites,
all migrated in this plan:
- `tests/test_task_numeric_bounds.py:511, 549, 927, 944, 980` (#337's eviction
  tests). Three are single-server and move to `_tasks.per_server`. Two are
  cross-server and move to `_tasks.total`: the liar/honest test at `:549`
  and the slow-clock test at `:927`. Under the new rule, a per-server cap
  cannot make one server evict another's records at all, so the only knob
  where their claim still means something is the total. Both pass unchanged
  in substance there (see Design decision 4).
- `tests/test_client_manager.py:1914`, `test_terminal_task_records_are_evicted_past_cap`.
  It records 1 active and 20 finished records with a cap of 5. On main 5
  finished records and the active one survive. Now the cap counts the active
  one too, so 4 finished records survive (`done-16`–`done-19`) and the
  active one stays. The expectation changes; the test's point, "finished go
  first and the active one survives", does not.
- `tests/test_client_manager.py:4501` writes `manager._tasks[...] = record`
  directly. The registry has no `__setitem__`, so it becomes
  `manager._tasks.put(record)`.

`tests/test_tools.py` uses a `MockClientManager` with its own `tasks` dict and
is unaffected (measured: 196 passed).

## Design decisions (made explicitly)

### 1. One way in: `TaskRegistry.put`, a read-only mapping

The brief prefers removing the degree of freedom over checking each path.
Main already has a single writer (`_record_task`), but nothing keeps it
single. `_tasks` is a `dict`, so any new line `self._tasks[k] = v` would
bypass the cap silently, and mypy would accept it.

So `_tasks` becomes a `TaskRegistry`, in a new module
`src/pmcp/client/task_registry.py`:
- It is a `collections.abc.Mapping`: `[]`, `get`, `in`, iteration, `len`,
  `items`. Every existing reader keeps working unchanged.
- It has **no** `__setitem__`, `__delitem__`, `pop`, `popitem`, `setdefault` or
  `update`. A write that bypasses `put` is a mypy error (measured: `Unsupported target
  for indexed assignment`) and a `TypeError` at runtime.
- `put(record)` stores the record and applies both caps before it returns.
  The cap is not something a path can forget to call: storing *is* capping.
- `drop_server(name)` handles a disconnect and `clear()` handles shutdown.
  These are the two removals main already has.

`_record_task` stays the one function that builds a record and calls `put`.
It keeps its #298 field logic unchanged. The structural tests in Design
decision 7 pin both facts.

The registry is a class rather than a closure in the manager for three
reasons. It can be tested directly against a reference model
(`test_the_registry_matches_a_plain_restatement_of_the_rule`). Its mutable
surface is its type. And the manager loses the 30 lines of
`_evict_terminal_tasks`.

### 2. Per server: 100 records, finished and unfinished alike

Each server has its own bucket, capped at `per_server = 100`. That is main's
number: on main, 100 was the limit a single busy server could already reach
for finished tasks. A bucket over its cap gives up **its own** records and
nothing else, so no downstream can evict another's.

### 3. Victim order inside a server: finished first, then unfinished, each in record order

Decided: **finished first.** Inside the server being evicted from:
1. its oldest finished record (`completed`, `failed` or `cancelled`), if it
   has one;
2. otherwise its oldest unfinished record. Any other status counts as
   unfinished, including `None` and unknown future statuses.

"Oldest" means least recently recorded by pmcp. That is the record order
#337 established (`_recorded_order`, pmcp's own sequence). A re-record, such
as a `tasks_get` refresh, makes a task the newest again. No downstream
timestamp plays any part.

The argument comes from the consumer table in the research summary:
- A finished record is a cache. Losing it costs a caller nothing they cannot
  get back: `tasks_get` and `tasks_result` still reach the downstream, and
  `tasks_list` falls back to the listed payload.
- An unfinished record carries behaviour that silently stops when it goes:
  - `tasks_cancel` refuses without asking the downstream;
  - the disconnect refusal and the forced-disconnect cancel skip it;
  - the stored `requestor_context` is lost.
- Pure record order would let a burst of finished tasks push out older
  unfinished ones. That is exactly the work a caller may still need to
  cancel.

The cost: a server that holds a few long-running unfinished tasks and churns
many finished ones keeps its unfinished tasks longer than pure record order
would. That is the intent. It cannot be abused across servers, because the
bucket is the server's own. A server flooding its own bucket with unfinished
tasks only evicts its own records: after 500, it keeps the newest 100
(repro (b)).

**The record `put` just stored is never a victim of that `put`.** This is
needed by `gateway.invoke`'s redaction default (research summary). The
consequence: a bucket full of unfinished records that receives a finished one
evicts its oldest unfinished record, not the newcomer
(`test_the_record_just_stored_is_never_its_own_victim`,
`test_invoke_at_the_cap_keeps_its_record_and_redacts_by_default`).

### 4. The total: 1000 records, taken from the server holding the most

`total = 1000`, which is ten servers at their cap. It is a backstop for
memory: below it, the per-server caps decide everything. Past it, one record
goes:
- from **the server holding the most records**;
- on a tie, from the server whose next victim (by the rule in decision 3) was
  recorded first;
- never the record just stored.

Why this rule rather than "the globally oldest record":
- **The largest server pays first.** A server stays at or below the others'
  size, and so never loses a record to the total while a larger one exists.
  Before the total is reached, no server loses anything to another.
- **The two #337 cross-server tests keep their meaning under it.**
  - `test_one_downstream_cannot_keep_its_tasks_by_claiming_a_future_timestamp`:
    the liar has 2, the honest server 1, and the total is 2. The liar is
    the largest, so its oldest record, `b1`, goes.
  - `test_an_honest_server_with_a_slow_clock_keeps_its_newer_records`: one
    record each, total 1. The new record is never taken, so the on-time
    server's `a` goes.

  Both migrated to the total knob and pass without changing an assertion
  (measured).

The cost is an O(records) scan when the total is exceeded: one
`_candidate` pass per server. That is at most 1000 records, once per `put`
past the total. Below the total, `put` is O(bucket) ≤ 100.

The caps are attributes on the registry (`per_server`, `total`), not
configuration. Tests lower them. Making them operator-configurable is a
non-goal: no one has asked, and main had a constant. A cap below 1 is
refused with `ValueError`.

### 5. An evicted unfinished task: what the caller sees

Eviction forgets pmcp's record, not the downstream's task. Pinned by
`test_an_evicted_unfinished_task_is_re_tracked_by_tasks_get`:

| Caller action | Result |
|---|---|
| the call that created it (`gateway.invoke`) | already returned; its output was built while the record existed |
| `gateway.tasks_get` | still proxied to the downstream; the reply is recorded again, as the newest, which can evict another of that server's records |
| `gateway.tasks_result` | the same, through its `task` payload or its `tasks/get` fallback |
| `gateway.tasks_list` | lists it if the downstream does, and records it again |
| `gateway.tasks_cancel` | `ok=false`, `status: "not_found"`, `Task not found: <server>::<id>`, as for a task pmcp never saw. **Recovery:** call `gateway.tasks_get` first, then cancel (pinned) |
| `gateway.disconnect_server` | not counted as active: an unforced disconnect is not refused for it, and a forced one sends no `tasks/cancel` for it, because no snapshot holds it. **A stdio server's task ends with its process; a remote server's task keeps running** after the disconnect (rev 3, panel N1). Main's `_remove_server_indexes` docstring claimed "the downstream's tasks died with the connection", which is false for a remote server, and rev 3 corrects it. A record evicted *during* a forced teardown is different: the teardown still cancels it, and a release returns it if the teardown is interrupted (Design decision 16) |
| a stored `requestor_context` | forgotten; the caller sends it again |

**Decided: `cancel_task` is unchanged.** It could forward an unknown task id
downstream instead of refusing. But that changes what `tasks_cancel` means
for *every* unknown id, not only evicted ones, and pmcp cannot tell "evicted"
from "never seen" without keeping tombstones, which is a second unbounded
structure. The recovery is one call, and the README now says so.

### 6. Disconnect, reconcile and shutdown keep main's semantics

- A **disconnect** drops all of that server's records: `drop_server(name)`
  replaces main's loop over every key at `:1644`. It also frees the
  server's share of the total (`test_a_disconnect_drops_only_that_servers_records_and_frees_its_count`).
- A **reconcile** (`drop_tasks=False`) keeps the records. This is main's
  existing test, `test_reconcile_preserves_tracked_task_records`, migrated
  to `put`.
- **Shutdown** clears the registry (`clear()`).
- A reconnect under the same name starts with an empty bucket, because every
  reconnect path goes through `_remove_server_indexes(name)` first.

### 7. Structural guards: one way in, a closed list of record paths

These are AST or type checks over `src/pmcp`, in the new module. Rev 2 adds
two more, for the read-then-await class, in Design decision 9.
- `test_record_task_is_called_by_exactly_the_tested_paths`: the set of
  functions that call `_record_task` must equal the set named in the
  per-path test table, `RECORD_PATHS`. A new record path fails this until it
  is added to that table, which also gives it a cap test (mutant M20).
- `test_only_record_task_builds_records_and_puts_them` scans **all of
  `src/pmcp`** (rev 2, N2):
  - `McpTaskRecord(` is constructed only in `_record_task`;
  - every `.put(` call in the package is classified, by file, function and
    receiver. There are exactly two: `self._tasks` in `_record_task`, and the
    npm resolver's `self._queue` in `_pump`.

  So an alias (`registry = self._tasks; registry.put(...)`) fails here, and
  so does any new `.put(`, until someone classifies it. That is an
  allowlist, not a denylist: an unrelated new queue costs one line in the
  test. Mutants M17, M19 and M35.
- `test_the_registry_is_written_in_exactly_three_places`: every use of
  `_tasks` in `src/pmcp` that is not a plain read (`get`, `items`, `[]`,
  `in`) must be one of four:
  - the binding in `__init__`;
  - `put` in `_record_task`;
  - `drop_server` in `_remove_server_indexes`;
  - `clear` in `_disconnect_all_unlocked`.

  `_tasks` is never passed or assigned as a value, so it has no alias
  (mutant M21).
- `test_the_registry_cannot_be_written_or_replaced_around_put`:
  - `TaskRegistry` has no mutating dunder or dict method;
  - an item assignment raises `TypeError`;
  - `self._tasks` is bound only in `__init__`, so nothing swaps in a dict
    (mutant M21).
- `test_every_record_path_is_capped` (parametrised over `RECORD_PATHS`,
  seven cases, one per row of the record-path table): each path is driven
  through the real manager method into a bucket already at its cap of 2.
  The test asserts:
  - that server's oldest record went;
  - the new record is present;
  - the other server's record is untouched.

mypy is a fifth guard, for free: a `self._tasks[...] = ...` anywhere in `src/`
is a type error once `_tasks` is a `Mapping`.

### 8. What is not changed

- the #298 field logic in `_record_task`: `created_at` stickiness,
  `unusable_fields` and `updated_at`;
- `_task_info_from_payload`;
- every handler except `refresh` (rev 2, Design decision 9);
- `cancel_task`'s and `gateway.tasks_cancel`'s answers (rev 3 splits the
  sending half of `cancel_task` into `_send_task_cancel`, with the same
  behaviour);
- the public output models. `_recorded_order` stays the private attribute
  #337 added; the registry now stamps it.

### 9. Every read-then-await site has a defined outcome for a gone record (rev 2; guards rev 3–5)

A record read before an await can be gone after it: evicted (anyone records
a task on that server, or a `tasks/cancel` reply names another id) or
dropped by a disconnect. Main had only the last.
- **Derived, not listed.** `test_every_snapshot_then_await_site_is_classified`
  scans `src/pmcp` for every function that reads tracked records before the
  end of one of its awaits and compares the set whole with
  `SNAPSHOT_SITES`, each with its outcome. A read is any reference to
  `get_active_tasks`, `get_tracked_tasks`, `get_task_record` or `_tasks`:
  called, aliased, passed as a bound method, a bare name or a `getattr`
  string (`test_the_snapshot_scan_sees_every_way_of_reading`, 10 fixture
  forms, the seats' aliases included). It is transitive through sync
  helpers and await-free `async` helpers (`_sync_readers`, pinned whole).
- **Outcomes.** The forced teardowns cancel an owed record whatever happens
  to it while they wait (Design decision 16). `get_task`,
  `get_task_result_with_task` and `cancel_task` pin their key (Design
  decision 15). Handlers count only, or use the reply's own record (Design
  decision 12).

### 10. `TaskRegistry` keys (rev 2, N3)

`__getitem__` raises `KeyError` for anything that is not a 2-tuple. That
covers a string: `"st"` would otherwise unpack to `("s", "t")` and *find* a
record. It also raises `KeyError` for a tuple with an unhashable part. So
`get` and `in` keep the `Mapping` contract: `None` and `False`, never
`ValueError` or `TypeError` (`test_a_key_that_is_not_a_pair_is_absent_not_an_error`,
six keys; mutants M32 and M33).

### 11. Task state is bound to the connection it came from (rev 5, round-4 codex F001)

Every outbound task request derived from stored state goes only to the
connection that state came from, and every stored record comes from the
server's current connection.
- `ManagedClient.connection_id` is unique for the process (an
  `itertools.count`, not `id()`); `McpTaskRecord._connection_id` (private)
  is stamped by `_record_task(..., connection=)`, keyword-only and required.
  A record built from a connection that is no longer the server's is
  returned but not stored.
- `_task_client(server, *, bound_to)` is the only way to get a connection
  for a `tasks/*` request; it refuses a record from a previous connection.
  A forced teardown checks the same (`_task_client_unavailable`) first, and
  skips the entry -- not counted, not an error -- if the snapshot's
  connection was removed, replaced, is reconnecting, or does not advertise
  tasks.
- Every registration of a connection drops the name's records first.
- Guards, compared whole: `TASK_SENDS` (every `tasks/*` send and the record
  it is bound to), the binding table (every value passed as `bound_to` or
  `connection`), `CLIENT_WRITES` (every write to `_clients`, by any means).

### 12. A handler uses this reply's record, never a lookup by key after an await (rev 6, round-5 F001)

`call_tool_with_task` and `get_task_result_with_task` return
`TaskReply(result, task)`, `task` being the record built from *this* reply
on *this* connection, stored or not (`None` for a reply about another
task). `gateway.invoke`, `gateway.tasks_result` and `gateway.tasks_list`
use it; `call_tool` and `get_task_result` are thin wrappers with their old
signatures. `READ_AFTER_AWAIT` lists, whole, every function that reads task
state after one of its awaits, with the reason it is safe. `gateway.invoke`
redacts by default only when a task was requested, as on main.

### 13. One connection per request, captured at entry (rev 7, round-6 F001/F002)

A request resolves its `ManagedClient` once, before its first await, and
every step uses that object: `_fetch_task(connection=)` resolves nothing;
the `get_task_result` fallback runs on the captured connection and only
while `_owns(server, managed)`; `list_tasks` and the teardown resolve once
per iteration (`RESOLVE_PER_ITERATION`). `_record_task` decides ownership
before it reads the registry, and merges only a same-connection record. The
resolution guard follows plain helpers; `_owns`, an identity check, is the
one exception.

### 14. Context precedence: supplied, else the existing record's (rev 8–9, round-7 codex F001)

`_record_task(..., requestor_context=)` is keyword-only with no default.
Only `call_tool_with_task` passes one -- `gateway.invoke`'s
`task.requestor_context` -- and it wins, as on main. Every reply and
listing passes `None`, and the existing same-connection record's context is
kept: in the registry, or, when the registry no longer holds the key, in a
running teardown's custody (`TaskRegistry.held`, Design decision 16).
`CONTEXT_FLOW` pins the value at every call (5). The residual since rev 3:
a task evicted while nothing pins or owes it loses its context, and the
caller sends it again (Design decision 5).

### 15. A record with a request in flight is never evicted (rev 9, round-8 F001)

`get_task`, `get_task_result_with_task` and `cancel_task` are decorated
with `@_pins_task`; a forced teardown pins each entry for its own
`tasks/cancel`. `ClientManager._pinned` is the only place pins are taken
or released (an `ExitStack` callback per key, so one failing release leaks
none), and it is used only as a `with` item. `TaskRegistry.pin` and `unpin`
keep a count per key; an unbalanced `unpin` raises. Eviction skips pinned
keys; releasing the last pin re-applies the caps at once. Since rev 12,
taking a pin on a key held only in a custody restores the record to the
registry first (Design decision 16).
### 16. Forced teardowns: what they owe, where it is held, who sees it, how it is released (rev 11–13)

A forced teardown is `_cancel_tracked_for_teardown(server_names)`, used by
`disconnect_server(force=True)` and `cancel_active_tasks` (a forced
refresh). Five rules, each a local check.

**R1. The obligation is fixed at the request.** The helper's first
statement reads every active task of every named server
(`get_active_tasks`, so including what other running teardowns owe), before
any await; neither caller awaits before calling it. Nothing that happens
while it waits can remove an entry. (Round 10, part 1.)

**R2. What it owes is held outside the caps, in a custody.**
`with self._custody(snapshot)` registers a `TaskCustody` (`watch`). The
registry keeps evicting as if no teardown ran; `_evict` hands each evicted
record to every custody that owes its key (`receive`), which replaces the
entry and never adds one. So overlapping teardowns cannot stack registry
retention (round 9). The teardown pins only the entry whose `tasks/cancel`
is in flight, like `cancel_task`. There is no lock (rev 10's lock, its
unpruned dict and its head-of-line wait are gone).

**R3. An owed task is a tracked task, for every reader.**
`get_tracked_tasks` -- the one source for "the tasks pmcp tracks" -- is the
registry plus `owed_records`: what custodies hold that the registry gave
up, once per key, on the server's current connection. Every reader goes
through it or `get_active_tasks`: the teardown snapshot, both disconnect
paths, the refresh gate, `mcp_tasks_seen`/`mcp_tasks_remaining`, and the
disconnect and restart counts. A second teardown therefore owes and cancels
what another owes, a disconnect without `force` refuses on it, and a
refresh counts it. `get_task_record` (one key, the registry's) is read only
under a pin, and a pin on a key held only in a custody restores the record
to the registry first, after the pin and on the current connection only
(`TaskRegistry.restore`): a record a request works on never lives only in a
custody, so get, result, cancel and the teardown send its context. A write
of an evicted owed key merges with the custody's record (`held`). The
structural guard `test_every_reader_of_task_state_goes_through_get_tracked_tasks`
pins every registry and `_custodies` access. (Round 11.)

**R4. Releasing a custody does not itself discard a task that was not
cancelled; an abort is not a discharge.** In `_custody`'s `finally`, after `unwatch`,
every record the custody holds that is not in the registry, not owed by
another running teardown, unfinished, and on its server's current
connection is restored to the registry, oldest first. That covers success
(everything was cancelled, finished or skipped for a gone connection, so
nothing is left), a cancelled caller, a downstream error, and a forced
disconnect of another server cancelling the in-flight request (round 12).
Back in the registry the record is an ordinary one: tracked, retriable,
visible to every gate, and under the caps. *(Implementation, round-13 N1
and N2:)* so the release does not guarantee survival: when the returned
records and the server's own exceed its cap, the cap evicts the excess in
its usual order. Each returned record ranks as the newest (oldest-owed
first), so it outlasts the server's older tasks, including newer-than-it
tasks no teardown ever owed; that priority is intended -- a task pmcp owed a
cancel outranks one it never owed. Only when the returned records alone
exceed the cap are the oldest of them evicted.

**R5. The cancel sent is the newest pmcp holds.** Per entry: the binding
check (Design decision 11); the pin, which restores; then finished →
counted, nothing sent; else `tasks/cancel` with the record's context
(Design decision 14). The "no record" branch is an explicit, unreachable
skip, not an `assert` (round-9 N1).

**The bounds.** With R(s) the `tasks/get`, `tasks/result` and
`tasks/cancel` requests on server s in flight *now* (each holds one pin; a
teardown's current cancel included) and R their sum, after every `put`,
`unpin` and `restore`, and so at every instant:

    records of server s <= max(per_server, R(s) + 1)
    all records         <= max(total, R + 1)

A custody is exactly its request-time snapshot. The tasks tracked only
through custodies are at most the owed records evicted during the current
unbroken chain of overlapping teardowns of a server: each a distinct
downstream task some teardown was asked to cancel and none has had
answered. That is history within the chain, not live concurrency; it is
zero once no teardown of the server runs. In codex's handoff loop (1,001
forced disconnects, each aborting the last, no cancel ever answered) the
registry peaks at 101 and, on this plan's base, the newest custody owed
1,100; the claude seat's 200-refresh chain: registry at its cap, one
custody of 209, all cancelled when the downstream answered. *(Implementation
on main after Consiliency/pmcp#324:* an interrupted forced disconnect still
disconnects, synchronously, so the handoff chain ends at the first handoff
and the newest custody owes 101; the chain bound is now tested on forced
refreshes, `test_a_chain_of_forced_refreshes_owes_its_history_and_cancels_it_all`.)

**Compared honestly with main.** Main never evicts an unfinished record:
its registry is unbounded in unfinished tasks (#338). Rev 13 bounds the
registry, so an unfinished task can be evicted -- before any teardown owes
it, or after a release returns it -- and is then untracked: a remote task
keeps running, a disconnect without `force` is not refused for it, and its
context is gone (Design decision 5, recoverable with `tasks_get`). That is
the cost of the cap, and it is the same for every unfinished task; what
rev 13 guarantees beyond the cap is that no teardown's *own* bookkeeping
loses a task: an owed task is never dropped by an eviction while owed or by
another teardown, and a release returns it to the registry (where only the
cap, as for any record, can evict it -- N1 above). Main is better for a flood of
unfinished tasks it can still hold in memory; rev 13 is better for a
downstream that floods without limit, which is the issue.

**Stated residuals.**
- A task recorded on a server after a forced disconnect of it started keeps
  running once the connection is removed, even if another teardown owes it
  (it then finds the connection gone). Main also leaves it running; main's
  `cancel_active_tasks` reports it as an error where rev 5 skips it.
- N1: a pin's restore takes a new `_recorded_order`, so it can evict
  another record at request start, even if the request then fails.
- N2: in a chain, each successor re-sends the cancels its predecessor sent
  (a repeated cancel is answered like any other).
- Discharge-on-send is not adopted: a sent `tasks/cancel` can still be lost
  when a forced disconnect tears the transport down before it is written.
- *(Implementation, Consiliency/pmcp#324 on main:)* a forced disconnect
  whose own teardown is interrupted still disconnects, synchronously,
  without the cancels it had not sent; a task of that server pmcp had not
  cancelled keeps running remotely, whichever teardown owed it. Main's
  `cancel_task` loop stops at the same point. The overlap harness exempts
  exactly those tasks: the ones not yet sent a `tasks/cancel` at the moment
  the interrupted disconnect removed the server (recorded then, by wrapping
  `_forget_disconnected`), never a whole server or a whole teardown.

**Tests.**
- Falsifiers, as written: round-10 codex (`test_teardown_snapshot_acceptance`),
  round-11 claude (`test_owed_task_in_custody_is_cancelled_despite_a_concurrent_disconnect`)
  and its non-forced variant, round-11 codex
  (`test_custody_context_survives_a_concurrent_poll`), round-12 claude
  (`test_a_disconnect_of_another_server_never_drops_an_owed_task`).
- `test_overlapping_teardowns_and_disconnects_never_drop_an_owed_task`: 9
  alphabets, each enumerated whole (3,960 orderings), over a forced refresh
  of both servers (T), forced disconnects of beta (D) and of alpha (X), a
  non-forced disconnect of beta (d), an abort of the newest teardown (A),
  floods of beta (F), `tasks/get` and `tasks/result` polls (G, R), a
  re-tracking listing (L) and answered requests (S). The test keeps its own
  model -- active tasks, contexts, what each teardown owed, what sits in a
  custody -- driven by events only (evictions, answers, and the moment a
  custody is released), never by reading the registry. For every ordering:
  the live and obligation bounds after every operation; every get, result
  and cancel carries the model's context; a disconnect without `force`
  never succeeds while a beta task runs; every task a completed teardown
  owed gets a `tasks/cancel`; every task an *aborted* one owed was answered,
  is still tracked, or was lost to the cap after its release (the model
  says nothing owed it then), or is the residual above; at the end every
  task the model tracks is tracked with its context; no pin or custody is
  left.
- `_LiveBounds` checks after every `put`, `unpin` and `watch`: both
  registry bounds with R read live; R never above the requests in flight;
  no custody changes size; the obligation bound.
- Release: `test_released_uncancelled_tasks_are_retried_and_refuse_a_plain_disconnect`,
  `test_a_custody_release_returns_only_uncancelled_current_unfinished_tasks`
  (cancelled, exception, reply), `test_a_release_returns_a_leftover_only_on_its_current_connection`,
  `test_a_release_never_returns_a_finished_task`,
  `test_released_leftovers_past_the_cap_keep_the_newest`.
- N4: `test_the_refresh_gate_and_its_counts_see_a_task_held_in_a_custody`
  (behavioural: a refresh without `force` counts and refuses on it; a
  forced one reports it remaining when nothing cancelled it).
- Restore, connection filter, dedupe, custody units, codex's handoff loop
  (registry 101; custody 101 on main after Consiliency/pmcp#324, 1,100 on
  the plan's base) and the 12 overlap variants, as in rev 12.

### 17. Superseded designs (one line each)

- Rev 1–2: the teardown used `cancel_task`, then skipped an evicted task --
  replaced by one sender and cancel-by-obligation.
- Rev 4–8: held-context fallbacks (`stored_context`, `_record_reply`,
  `fallback_context`) -- replaced by pinning (rev 9).
- Rev 9: the teardown pinned its whole snapshot -- unbounded under handoffs
  (round 9).
- Rev 10: a per-server teardown lock, snapshot read under it -- missed
  owed tasks (round 10).
- Rev 11: custody invisible to other readers (round 11); rev 12: custody
  released with its contents on abort (round 12).
- Weighed, not adopted: owed records kept in the registry under a separate
  budget (rev 9's mechanism; fails round-10 codex part 2); discharge on
  send; no custody with a hard bound on unfinished records (rev 13 header).

## Documentation impact

### The three #337 CHANGELOG corrections, each checked against the code

1. **"A `NaN` inside a tool result now reaches the caller as a `NaN` token."**
   The issue says this applies to every relayed downstream value. I
   measured it on the spike (`nan_relay.py`, reproduced
   below). The script uses the real `ClientManager` and `GatewayTools`, puts
   a `NaN` in each reply, and serialises the output with the gateway's own
   encoder, `json.dumps(result.model_dump(), indent=2)`
   (`server.py:450-467`):

   | Gateway tool | Where the NaN was | `NaN` token in the caller's output |
   |---|---|---|
   | `gateway.invoke` | tool result | **yes** |
   | `gateway.tasks_result` | `result` | **yes** |
   | `gateway.tasks_get` | task `raw` | no (`raw` is nulled, as #337's entry says) |
   | `gateway.tasks_list` | task `raw` | no |

   Resource reads (`server.py:618-630`) and prompt gets (`:688-702`) pass on
   only `uri`, `mimeType` and `text` strings, so no number from the
   downstream reaches the caller there.

   So "not just tool results" is right, but "every relayed value" is too
   broad. The corrected text names both paths that relay a value as sent,
   and the two that do not.
2. **"a string where a number is expected"**. Measured with
   `_task_info_from_payload`:
   - `ttl: "5"` and `pollInterval: "5"` are unusable;
   - `createdAt: "5"`, `createdAt: "2026-01-02T03:04:05Z"`,
     `lastUpdatedAt: "5.5"` and the same ISO string are **kept**: 5.0,
     1767323045.0, 5.5 and 1767323045.0;
   - `lastUpdatedAt: "soon"` is unusable.

   The source is `_usable_task_timestamp` (`types.py:563-596`): "a numeric
   string or an ISO 8601 string". The bullet becomes: a string for `ttl` or
   `pollInterval` (a numeric or ISO 8601 string is still accepted for
   `createdAt` and `lastUpdatedAt`/`updatedAt`).
3. **"now reports `updatedAt: null`"**. `McpTaskInfo` declares `updated_at`
   and no alias (`types.py:633`). A recorded task with an unusable
   `lastUpdatedAt` dumps the key `updated_at` with value `None`, and `raw`
   keeps the wire key `lastUpdatedAt` (measured). The text becomes
   `updated_at: null`.

The #337 bullet on eviction ("Finished tasks past the 100-record cap are now
evicted in the order pmcp last recorded them …") stays true. It gains one
sentence pointing to the #338 entry, which follows it in the same
`### Changed` section. That section is still under `[Unreleased]`.

### The new entry, README and SECURITY

These are given verbatim in the patch. Each states the bound (100 per server,
1000 in total) and the victim order. The entries also cover what happens to
an evicted task (`tasks_get` tracks it again; cancel needs that first; a
disconnect neither waits for it nor cancels it; send `requestor_context`
again) and the fact that `tasks_list` still returns everything listed.
`python3 scripts/check_security_claims.py` stays `OK` (129 cited node ids).

## Dependencies & order

1. Independent of every open plan. It applies to `31c1357` as is.
2. It **composes with Consiliency/pmcp#335** (NaN in relayed results). That
   plan may reword correction 1 again; the text here is what the code does on
   `31c1357`.
3. Within this plan: write the registry, then switch `manager.py` to it, then
   migrate the 7 test sites, then add the new module, then the docs.

## Verification

Run from a fresh worktree of `origin/main` on dev0 (a team host).

```bash
git -C ~/code/pmcp worktree add -b fix/338-task-cap "$WORKTREE_ROOT/pmcp-338-fix" origin/main
cd "$WORKTREE_ROOT/pmcp-338-fix"
uv sync --all-extras -p 3.10
UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT=.venv-3.11 uv sync --all-extras -p 3.11
UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT=.venv-3.12 uv sync --all-extras -p 3.12
mkdir -p /var/tmp/pmcp-338-bt-$USER
```

Apply *Verbatim bodies* (How to apply), then:

```bash
BT=/var/tmp/pmcp-338-bt-$USER
REL="tests/test_task_tracking_cap.py tests/test_task_numeric_bounds.py tests/test_client_manager.py \
     tests/test_tools.py tests/test_phase4_e2e.py tests/test_gateway_tool_schemas.py \
     tests/test_integration.py tests/test_lazy_start.py tests/test_scoped_advisor_audit.py"
# 1. CI gates
.venv/bin/ruff check src/ tests/ && .venv/bin/ruff format --check src/ tests/
.venv/bin/mypy src/pmcp --exclude baml_client
.venv/bin/python scripts/check_security_claims.py
# 2. the relevant suites on 3.10, 3.11 and 3.12 (npm vars unset: dev0 needs it for test_tools)
for v in .venv .venv-3.11 .venv-3.12; do
  env -u npm_config_cache -u npm_config_store_dir $v/bin/python -m pytest $REL -q -p no:cacheprovider --basetemp=$BT/rel${v#.venv}
done
# 3. the full suite, once
env -u npm_config_cache -u npm_config_store_dir .venv/bin/python -m pytest -q -p no:cacheprovider --basetemp=$BT/full
# 4. the mutation table (Verbatim bodies), from the tree root, alone on its tree
env -u npm_config_cache -u npm_config_store_dir .venv/bin/python -u mutants.py "$PWD"
```

## Acceptance criteria

**The cap (rev 1–3).**
- [ ] One server's records never evict another's below the total: a listing
  of 98, 99, 100 or 500 leaves B's two finished tasks
  (`test_one_server_listing_tasks_never_evicts_another_servers`).
- [ ] A server keeps at most 100 records, finished and unfinished, for
  `working`, `input_required` and every status #298 nulls (6 cases).
- [ ] Victims: finished before unfinished, each in record order; a
  re-record is the newest (5 tests). The total is 1000 and takes from the
  largest server, ties to the oldest victim, never the new record; the
  registry agrees with a list-scan restatement over 3000 random puts.
- [ ] Every record path goes through `put` (`test_every_record_path_is_capped`,
  7 cases; the callers of `_record_task` equal that table). Only
  `_record_task` builds a record or calls `put`; the registry cannot be
  written around `put` or rebound; non-pair keys are absent.
- [ ] `gateway.invoke` at the cap keeps its record and redacts by default;
  `gateway.tasks_list` returns all 500 listed. An evicted unfinished task:
  `tasks_cancel` says not found, `tasks_get` re-tracks it, then cancel works.
- [ ] A disconnect drops only that server's records; a reconcile keeps them.
  A forced refresh that cannot disconnect a server reports it.

**Isolation and context (rev 4–9).**
- [ ] No stored task id or context is sent on a connection other than its
  own; a reply from a replaced connection is not stored, read, or merged;
  every `tasks/*` send, binding and `_clients` write matches its table
  (Design decisions 11–13).
- [ ] Handlers use the reply's own record; no function reads task state
  after an await unless listed in `READ_AFTER_AWAIT`.
- [ ] Context precedence is supplied, else existing; `CONTEXT_FLOW` (5)
  matches every `_record_task` call; the newest context wins in every
  order of the 90-case generated test.
- [ ] A record with a request in flight is never evicted; pins are released
  on reply, exception, cancellation and timeout (16 cases plus the autouse
  leak guard, which also covers custodies); pins are taken only in
  `_pinned`.

**Forced teardowns (rev 11–13, Design decision 16).**
- [ ] R1: the obligation is the helper's first statement, all servers, no
  await before it (structural); round-10 codex's falsifier passes as
  written (`beta::b` cancelled, `(2, [])`; 100 retained at R = 0).
- [ ] R2: a custody never grows; the teardown pins one entry at a time; no
  lock.
- [ ] R3: every reader sees a custody-held task on its server's current
  connection (structural guard; round-11 claude's falsifier and its
  non-forced variant; N4's behavioural refresh test); a pin restores it,
  after the pin and on the current connection only (round-11 codex's
  falsifier: both polls and the later cancel carry `{'tenant': 'b'}`).
- [ ] R4: releasing a custody returns every uncancelled, unfinished owed
  record on its current connection, oldest first, on success, cancellation,
  exception and a cross-server disconnect (round-12 claude's falsifier:
  `b1` and `b2` tracked again with their contexts; a non-forced disconnect
  of beta refuses; a retry cancels them); never a finished one or another
  connection's.
- [ ] R5 and the bounds: after every `put`, `unpin` and `watch`, a server
  holds at most `max(per_server, R(s) + 1)` and the registry
  `max(total, R + 1)`, R read live; the obligation bound holds; codex's
  handoff loop gives a registry peak of 101 and, on main after
  Consiliency/pmcp#324 (the first interrupted forced disconnect removes the
  server), a custody of exactly 101; a chain of forced refreshes owes its
  whole history (10 + 49) and cancels it all
  (`test_a_chain_of_forced_refreshes_owes_its_history_and_cancels_it_all`).
- [ ] All 3,960 orderings of the overlap harness pass, with aborts modelled
  precisely: no teardown's obligation is exempted wholesale. The only
  exemptions are the stated residuals, each scoped to individual tasks: a
  task recorded after a forced disconnect of its server started, and a task
  that had not been sent a `tasks/cancel` at the moment an interrupted
  forced disconnect removed its server (Consiliency/pmcp#324), recorded at
  that moment.

**Process.**
- [ ] The three #337 CHANGELOG corrections read as in *Documentation impact*.
- [ ] ruff, ruff format, mypy and `check_security_claims.py` are clean; the
  relevant suites pass on 3.10, 3.11 and 3.12; the full suite passes once.
- [ ] Every mutant in the table is killed under the strict rule (pytest
  exit 1 with a failing test named).
- [ ] `scripts/check_plan_consistency.py` reports no blocking inconsistency;
  the plan stays under 420 KB.

## Mutation table

`mutants.py` (in *Verbatim bodies*) applies one string replacement at a
time to the spike, runs seven modules with `-x` (`test_task_tracking_cap`,
`test_task_numeric_bounds`, `test_client_manager`, `test_tools`,
`test_phase4_e2e`, `test_integration`, `test_lazy_start`) with the npm
variables unset, then restores the file from a copy in memory, never
through `git`, and stops the run if a restore does not match its saved
bytes. **The kill rule (rev 12):** a mutant is killed only when pytest
exits 1 and reports a `FAILED tests/…` or `ERROR tests/…` line; a
collection, internal or usage error is `NOT KILLED`. A pattern that does
not match exactly once is reported, never skipped.

**Measured on rev 13 (`6db13ca`): 107 of 107 killed**, one clean run alone on its
own tree; no `PATTERN COUNT` misses, no restore failures. The first-failure
column below is that run's. Rev 13 adds M114–M118 (the release rule).

**Behavioural pass.** The teardown and custody mutants (M27, M83, M89–M91,
M93–M95, M99–M113) were re-run in rev 12 with every structural test
deselected: all killed. M106 and M107 were then killed only incidentally;
in rev 13, re-run the same way with M93 and M114–M118 (8 of 8 killed), they
fall to `test_the_refresh_gate_and_its_counts_see_a_task_held_in_a_custody`
(N4), and M114 to the overlap harness (`abort+get`). Rev 7's six argued survivors of that pass
stand: M49, M68 and M70 are equivalent under the ordering; M63, M64 and
M71 are guard mutants.

**Retired, one line each:** M2 (equivalent: an overflowing server is the
largest); M24, M29, M38, M42a–i, M73 and M74 (rev 5–8 context code
removed by pinning in rev 9); M86 (rev 10's lock) and M88 (rev 10's N1
`assert`), removed in rev 11; M96–M98 (rev 11's custody fallback), removed
in rev 12.

## Non-goals

- **Configurable caps.** Main had a constant; nobody has asked.
- **Forwarding `tasks_cancel` for an unknown id**, or tombstones of evicted
  ids (Design decision 5).
- **Bounding one `tasks/list` reply.** The cap bounds what pmcp tracks.
- **NaN in relayed results** (Consiliency/pmcp#335) and **`ttl` expiry**
  (Consiliency/pmcp#330).

## Unverified

- **Memory at the total.** 1000 records each hold a downstream `raw` dict:
  bounded in count, not bytes -- the same exposure as main, now capped.
- **Memory running teardowns hold.** Across a chain of overlapping forced
  teardowns of one server in which no cancel is answered, the newest
  custody grows with the chain's history (a chain of forced refreshes; a
  chain of forced disconnects ends at the first interrupted one on main
  after Consiliency/pmcp#324, custody 101). Each entry is a real downstream task pmcp was asked to cancel;
  it is released (to the registry, R4) when the chain ends.
- **Real downstream servers.** Every path was driven through the real
  manager and handlers with a canned `_send_request`; #337's transport
  tests cover the wire.

## Execution Policy

- execute: effort=low.
- reason: one new module and the manager's task paths; no public field.
  Behaviour changes: the cap; finished-first eviction, which can evict an
  unfinished record; forced teardowns that owe, see, cancel and return
  through the rules of Design decision 16; refresh reporting a failed
  disconnect. Tests, structural guards and 107 killed mutants pin them.
- Re-run the mutation table, the relevant suites on 3.10–3.12, ruff and mypy
  before requesting review. Cross-vendor panel CR before merge.

## Verbatim bodies

### How to apply

Every body below sits between four-backtick fences. Apply them to `31c1357`,
or to any later main where `git apply --check` passes:

1. Save *Patch* as `338.patch`, then `git apply 338.patch`. It modifies
   `src/pmcp/client/manager.py`, `src/pmcp/tools/handlers.py`,
   `src/pmcp/types.py`, `CHANGELOG.md`, `README.md`, `SECURITY.md` and seven
   test modules, and creates `tests/task_reply_double.py`.
2. Write *File — `src/pmcp/client/task_registry.py`* to that path.
3. Write *File — `tests/test_task_tracking_cap.py`* to that path.
4. Run *Verification*.

The two scripts at the end are not part of the change: `nan_relay.py` (the
evidence for #337's CHANGELOG correction 1) and `mutants.py` (the mutation
runner). Rev 12's seven repro scripts are retired: every finding they
reproduced is a test in the module (*History*), and those tests run in
*Verification*.

### Embedding proof

Measured for rev 13 on main `31c1357`, in a fresh detached worktree of main
(`$WORKTREE_ROOT/pmcp-338-embed`; not this plan's worktree), with no other
change in the tree:
1. The three bodies below were extracted from a draft of **this file**, not
   from the spike (the first four-backtick block after each heading:
   1523, 377 and 5138 lines), and are byte for byte the bodies in this final
   file (re-extracted from the committed file, compared with `cmp`).
2. `git apply --check 338.patch` and `git apply 338.patch` passed; the two
   files were then written.
3. The tree is **byte-identical to the rev 13 spike `6db13ca`**:
   `git diff --quiet spike/338 --` passed on the 13 modified files, a
   whole-tree `git diff spike/338` lists only the three new files, and
   `cmp` passed on those three.
4. On that tree: `ruff check`, `ruff format --check` and `mypy` clean; the
   9 relevant modules **1502 passed, 5 deselected (109.4 s)**; `nan_relay.py` as in *Documentation
   impact*. The full suite ran on the spike, the same tree.
5. The embedding worktree was then removed.

### Patch — `src/pmcp/{client/manager.py, tools/handlers.py, types.py}`, the migrated test modules and doubles, `tests/task_reply_double.py`, `CHANGELOG.md`, `README.md`, `SECURITY.md`

````diff
diff --git a/CHANGELOG.md b/CHANGELOG.md
index 0ae8ba2..5cb0acd 100644
--- a/CHANGELOG.md
+++ b/CHANGELOG.md
@@ -605,7 +605,8 @@ and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0
     `unusable_fields` array. "Cannot use" covers:
     - non-finite numbers and out-of-range values;
     - a boolean;
-    - a string where a number is expected;
+    - a string for `ttl` or `pollInterval` (a numeric or ISO 8601 string is
+      still accepted for `createdAt` and `lastUpdatedAt`/`updatedAt`);
     - a non-string or blank status;
     - a blank timestamp;
     - a `null` the downstream sent.
@@ -619,7 +620,7 @@ and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0
     `updatedAt` no longer hides a good `lastUpdatedAt`. The task's `raw` holds
     what the downstream sent, except that non-finite numbers appear there as
     `null`. A task whose `lastUpdatedAt` was unusable now reports
-    `updatedAt: null` instead of the time pmcp recorded it. A downstream that
+    `updated_at: null` instead of the time pmcp recorded it. A downstream that
     leaves the field out entirely still gets pmcp's observation time, as
     before.
   - Over HTTP and SSE, downstream replies are now read as the server sent
@@ -628,14 +629,18 @@ and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0
     - **A `nextCursor` of `NaN` now makes that listing unreadable.** pmcp keeps
       the previous tools/resources/prompts and publishes no change, instead of
       treating page one as the whole listing.
-    - **A `NaN` inside a tool result now reaches the caller as a `NaN` token
-      instead of `null`.** Fixing that for every transport is tracked as
-      Consiliency/pmcp#335.
+    - **A `NaN` in a downstream value that pmcp relays as sent now reaches the
+      caller as a `NaN` token instead of `null`.** That is a tool result
+      (`gateway.invoke`) and a task result (`gateway.tasks_result`), not only
+      the first. A task's `raw` still shows `null`, and resource and prompt
+      reads pass on only their text. Fixing that for every transport is
+      tracked as Consiliency/pmcp#335.
   - Finished tasks past the 100-record cap are now evicted in the order pmcp
     last recorded them. A downstream's own timestamps play no part: a
     far-future `lastUpdatedAt` can no longer keep one server's tasks while
     another's are dropped, and a server whose clock runs behind no longer
-    loses its tasks first.
+    loses its tasks first. Consiliency/pmcp#338 then made the cap per server
+    (next entry).
   - pmcp no longer sends a downstream server anything that is not strict JSON:
     `NaN`, `±Infinity`, or a value JSON cannot encode. A request whose
     `arguments` or task metadata contain one now fails with `outbound frame is
@@ -646,6 +651,97 @@ and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0
   - **Known follow-up:** pmcp documents `ttl` and `poll_interval` in seconds,
     but MCP defines both in milliseconds, and pmcp forwards them unchanged.
     Tracked as Consiliency/pmcp#330.
+- **Tracked MCP tasks are capped per server, and the cap now covers
+  unfinished tasks (see Consiliency/pmcp#338).**
+  - pmcp tracks at most 100 tasks per downstream server, finished and
+    unfinished alike, and at most 1000 across all servers. Before, one
+    100-record cap was shared by every server and counted only finished
+    tasks. So a server whose tasks a caller listed could evict another
+    server's finished tasks, and a server could keep any number of records
+    alive by reporting them as `working`, or with a status pmcp cannot use.
+  - Past a server's cap, that server gives up its own records: finished ones
+    first, then unfinished ones, each in the order pmcp last recorded them.
+    Past the total, the server holding the most records gives one up.
+    Recording a task never evicts that same task, so `gateway.invoke` keeps
+    the record of the task it just started. A listing is recorded one task at
+    a time, so a `tasks/list` reply longer than the cap keeps only its last
+    100 tasks.
+  - Eviction forgets pmcp's record, not the downstream's task.
+    `gateway.tasks_get` and `gateway.tasks_result` still reach it and track
+    it again. Until then:
+    - `gateway.tasks_cancel` reports it as not found;
+    - it does not count as an active task;
+    - a disconnect neither waits for it nor cancels it. A stdio server's task
+      ends with its process. **A remote server's task keeps running** after
+      the disconnect, until the downstream ends it;
+    - a `requestor_context` pmcp stored with it is forgotten, so send it
+      again.
+  - `gateway.tasks_list` still returns every task the downstream listed,
+    even past the cap.
+  - A task that a request is working on is never evicted while that request
+    is in flight: `gateway.tasks_get`, `gateway.tasks_result`,
+    `gateway.tasks_cancel`, and the one `tasks/cancel` a forced teardown has
+    in flight. While such requests are in flight, a server may hold more than
+    100 records: at most one more than the requests in flight at that moment,
+    and it is back under its cap as soon as they finish. The 1000 total works
+    the same way.
+  - A forced `gateway.disconnect_server`, `gateway.restart_server` or
+    `gateway.refresh` cancels every task that was active on the server when
+    it was requested, with each task's newest `requestor_context`, so a
+    remote downstream still gets it. What it owes is fixed when it starts: a
+    task the cap evicts while the teardown works through the others is still
+    cancelled, and still with its context. Those tasks do not count against
+    the caps, so overlapping forced disconnects cannot keep growing a server
+    past its cap, but they still count as the server's active tasks: another
+    forced teardown cancels them too, a disconnect without `force` is refused
+    while any remains, and `gateway.refresh` counts them. A teardown that is
+    interrupted -- its caller cancelled, a downstream error, or a forced
+    disconnect of another server -- puts every task it did not cancel back
+    among the tracked ones, so a retry cancels them. Eviction never fails the
+    teardown.
+    One kind of snapshot task is skipped: one whose original connection
+    cannot take a task request at that moment. That covers four cases: a
+    concurrent disconnect removed the server, a reconnect has it listed but
+    not yet connected, a reconnect replaced it with a new connection, or it
+    does not advertise task support. No `tasks/cancel` is sent for such a
+    task, and it is not an error. On a
+    remote server that is reconnecting, the task is not cancelled.
+    `gateway.disconnect_server` and `gateway.restart_server` still include it
+    in `cancelled_task_count`. That field counts the active tasks pmcp no
+    longer tracks after the call (before minus after), as it always has.
+    Reporting the cancels actually sent is a separate change (see
+    Consiliency/pmcp#349).
+  - When pmcp records a downstream reply about a task (`tasks/get`,
+    `tasks/result`, `tasks/cancel`), the task keeps the `requestor_context`
+    pmcp holds for it. That is the newest one: if `gateway.invoke` supplied a
+    newer context for the same task while the request was in flight, the
+    reply keeps that one. A later cancel sends it. A reply that names a
+    different task does not take this task's context.
+  - Task state is bound to the connection it came from. A reconnect drops the
+    server's tracked tasks, as before. A reply that arrives on the old
+    connection after a reconnect is no longer recorded. pmcp never sends a
+    stored task id or `requestor_context` on a connection other than the one
+    the task came from, so a reconnect to another URL or tenant cannot
+    receive the old tenant's context, or cancel an unrelated task that has
+    the same id. For a record from a previous connection,
+    `gateway.tasks_get`, `gateway.tasks_result` and `gateway.tasks_cancel`
+    fail with `Server <name> was reconnected; this task belongs to its
+    previous connection`, and a forced teardown skips it. `gateway.invoke`,
+    `gateway.tasks_result` and `gateway.tasks_list` report the task the way
+    *this* reply describes it. They never report a same-id task that a new
+    connection tracks, which could belong to another tenant. They also never
+    merge its `created_at`, `tool_id` or `requestor_context`. Each request
+    uses one connection, captured when it starts. If that connection is
+    replaced while `tasks/result` is in flight, its `tasks/get` fallback is
+    not sent anywhere else: `gateway.tasks_result` returns the result with
+    no task. `gateway.invoke`
+    still redacts by default when the call requested a task and the reply
+    carries one.
+  - A forced `gateway.refresh` that cannot disconnect a server now says so.
+    It returns `ok: false` with
+    `Server '<name>' could not be disconnected: <reason>`. Before, it
+    reported success, and the server stayed connected even if it had been
+    removed from the config or denied by policy.
 - **`pmcp startup add/set --source project` now carries your prior trust approval forward when it rewrites `.mcp.json`.** Setting the startup policy changes the file's bytes, and trust approval is content-keyed, so the edit used to silently invalidate your own `pmcp trust approve` of that file and the next startup refused it. When the pre-write bytes were approved, pmcp now re-records the approval for the exact bytes it writes — keyed on the opened descriptor's verified identity (the resolved key must name the same file the descriptor holds open), never re-approving a file that was not already approved, and never approving a substituted file. A target swapped or unlinked mid-operation is refused rather than mis-bound, and on POSIX a symlinked `.mcp.json` is refused up front. If re-recording ever fails because the trust store is unusable, the edit is still written and the failure is surfaced as a diagnostic rather than crashing (an unusable store also fails the approval check, so nothing is silently carried forward). See [#253](https://github.com/Consiliency/pmcp/issues/253).
 - **`pmcp trust approve` now refuses a store resident in the checkout containing the file being approved**, matching what `serve --project` enforces — so approve no longer reports success for an approval that serve will then refuse. See [#252](https://github.com/Consiliency/pmcp/issues/252).
 - **Every install spawn now logs the command it runs, at WARNING, before it
diff --git a/README.md b/README.md
index cfdd1b2..6c1e8bb 100644
--- a/README.md
+++ b/README.md
@@ -1674,7 +1674,13 @@ Hosted operators should require Bearer auth on `/mcp`, tune `--rate-limit` or
 network controls. `gateway.refresh`, `gateway.disconnect_server`, and
 `gateway.restart_server` can disrupt in-flight downstream work unless forced by
 policy; use downstream task IDs with `gateway.tasks_cancel` for tenant run
-cancellation. PMCP task records are transient. Durable sandbox logs, artifacts,
+cancellation. PMCP task records are transient, and bounded: at most 100 per
+server and 1000 in total. To cancel a task pmcp no longer tracks, call
+`gateway.tasks_get` first, which tracks it again. A forced disconnect,
+restart or refresh cancels only the tasks pmcp still tracks when it starts.
+On a remote tenant server, a task that was evicted before then keeps running
+after the disconnect. Cancel it explicitly first, with `gateway.tasks_get`
+and then `gateway.tasks_cancel`. Durable sandbox logs, artifacts,
 tenant authorization, and artifact retention remain responsibilities of the
 companion tenant server and its deployment controls.

 - **MCP task records are transient**: task IDs are downstream server identifiers

-  audit records and do not provide cross-user authorization isolation on
-  unauthenticated local transports.
+  held in gateway memory for visibility and cancellation. pmcp keeps at most

+        `force` refuses on it. Back in the registry it is an ordinary record,
+        under the caps like any other (oldest first, so the newest survive)."""

+        try:

+                    if key not in self._tasks

+                    and not self._terminal_task(record)
+                    and self._on_current_connection(record)

+                key=lambda record: record._recorded_order,
+            )
+            for record in leftovers:

+        await (rev 7, structurally pinned)."""

diff --git a/tests/task_reply_double.py b/tests/task_reply_double.py
new file mode 100644
index 0000000..2914a13
--- /dev/null
+++ b/tests/task_reply_double.py
@@ -0,0 +1,37 @@
+"""Test-double support for `ClientManager.call_tool_with_task` and
+`get_task_result_with_task` (Consiliency/pmcp#338, rev 6).
+
+`gateway.invoke` and `gateway.tasks_result` take the task record the manager
+built from *this* reply instead of looking it up by `(server, task_id)` after
+the await. A client-manager double that already defines `call_tool`,
+`get_task_result` and `get_task_record` mixes this in to answer the new
+calls. Its "record built from this reply" is its own record of the task the
+reply names, which is what a double's registry holds.
+"""
+
+from __future__ import annotations
+
+from typing import Any
+
+from pmcp.client.manager import TaskReply
+
+
+class TaskReplyDouble:
+    async def call_tool_with_task(
+        self, tool_id: str, args: dict[str, Any], timeout_ms: int = 30000, **kwargs: Any
+    ) -> TaskReply:
+        result = await self.call_tool(tool_id, args, timeout_ms, **kwargs)  # type: ignore[attr-defined]
+        task = None
+        payload = result.get("task") if isinstance(result, dict) else None
+        if isinstance(payload, dict):
+            task_id = payload.get("taskId") or payload.get("task_id")
+            lookup = getattr(self, "get_task_record", None)
+            if isinstance(task_id, str) and lookup is not None:
+                task = lookup(tool_id.split("::", 1)[0], task_id)
+        return TaskReply(result, task)
+
+    async def get_task_result_with_task(
+        self, server_name: str, task_id: str, **kwargs: Any
+    ) -> TaskReply:
+        result = await self.get_task_result(server_name, task_id, **kwargs)  # type: ignore[attr-defined]
+        return TaskReply(result, self.get_task_record(server_name, task_id))  # type: ignore[attr-defined]

-        """Terminal task records are pruned past the cap; active ones survive."""
+        """The per-server cap counts every record; finished ones are evicted
+        first, so the active one survives (Consiliency/pmcp#338)."""
         manager = ClientManager()
-        manager._max_terminal_tasks = 5

             "srv",
             McpTaskInfo(task_id="active", status="working", updated_at=0.0),
+            requestor_context=None,
+            connection=None,
         )
 
         # Record many terminal tasks with increasing updated_at timestamps.
@@ -1926,15 +1931,17 @@ class TestCallTool:

             t for t in manager.get_tracked_tasks("srv") if manager._terminal_task(t)
         ]
-        assert len(terminal) == 5

         # Oldest terminal records were dropped; newest survive.
````
